\mathbb{Q}[\underline{x},\underline{y},\nu,u]\llbracket t\rrbracket
.

Figure 12
.
A black-rooted plane map equipped with a spin configuration, in which all vertices have degree 4. Monochromatic edges are represented by solid lines, while frustrated edges are shown as dashed lines.

Remark 4.1
.

The variable
t
is redundant in the following sense:

I_{\circ}(\underline{x},\underline{y},t,\nu,u)=I_{\circ}\Big((x_{k}\,t^{k/2})_{k\geq 1},\,(y_{k}\,t^{k/2})_{k\geq 1},\,1,\,\nu,\,u\Big).

This results from enumerating the edges via the degrees of the vertices in maps. Still, in the rest of this section, the use of the variable
t
will simplify subsequent discussions.

4.2.
Connection with bipartite maps

In this section, we recall the well-known correspondence between maps endowed with a spin configuration and bipartite maps; see
[
27
,
14
,
16
]
, and see Figure
13
for an example.

We consider bipartite maps where vertices of degree 2 can either be square or round, while all other vertices are round. The correspondence between maps with a spin configuration and bipartite maps (which is many-to-one) operates as follows: for a given map with a spin configuration, each edge is replaced by a chain, of arbitrary length, made of square vertices of degree two, in such a way that the resulting map is bipartite. Specifically, monochromatic edges are replaced with chains containing an odd number of vertices, whereas frustrated edges are replaced by chains with an even number of vertices.

Reciprocally, for a bipartite map containing square vertices of degree two, the corresponding map with a spin configuration is obtained by contracting each maximal chain of square vertices into a single edge.

In order to translate this correspondence into a relation for generating functions, we first introduce the following notation. Let
\mathcal{M}_{\circ}^{{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{262144}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\hbox to5.92pt{\vbox to5.92pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.39833pt\lower-0.39833pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{

{}{{}}{}
{}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 0 0 L 0 7.09 L 7.09 7.09 L 7.09 0 Z M 7.09 7.09}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}

\displaystyle\coloneqq t^{\#\{\text{maximal chains of squares}\}}\nu^{\#\{\text{squares}\}}u^{\#\mathrm{F}(\mathrm{m})}\prod_{v\in\mathrm{V}_{\circ}(\mathrm{m})}x_{\deg(v)}\prod_{v\in\mathrm{V}_{\bullet}(\mathrm{m})}y_{\deg(v)},

\displaystyle{\ =t^{\#\{\text{maximal chains of squares}\}}\nu^{\#\{\text{squares}\}}\,w(\mathrm{m})},

\displaystyle=t^{\#\mathrm{E}(\mathrm{m})}(\nu/t)^{\#\{\text{squares}\}}\,w(\mathrm{m}).

Recall that, in the above display,
w
denotes the weight for bipartite planar maps defined in (
3.6
).

Next, we define the following key change of variables that will link the generating functions of bipartite maps and Ising maps:

Definition 4.2
.

Let
\Theta
be the change of variables on
\mathbb{Q}\llbracket\underline{x},\underline{y},t,\nu,u\rrbracket
defined by:

\Theta\Big(A\big(\underline{x},\underline{y},t,\nu,u\big)\Big)\coloneqq A\Big(\underline{x},\underline{y},\frac{t}{1-\nu^{2}},\nu,u\Big),

for any
A\in\mathbb{Q}\llbracket\underline{x},\underline{y},t,\nu,u\rrbracket
.

Figure 13
.
A bipartite map
\tilde{\mathrm{m}}
with round vertices of arbitrary degree and square vertices of degree 2 (left). Its weight is
{w(\tilde{\mathrm{m}})=x_{2}x_{3}x_{4}y_{4}y_{5}t^{9}\nu^{16}u^{6}}
.
Its corresponding map endowed with a spin configuration
(\mathrm{m},\sigma)
obtained by contracting the chains of square vertices. Its weight is
{w^{\mathrm{Ising}}(\mathrm{m},\sigma)=x_{2}x_{3}x_{4}y_{4}y_{5}t^{9}\nu^{2}u^{6}}
(right).

All together, the one-to-many correspondence between bipartite maps and maps endowed with a spin configuration leads to the following:

(4.4)

In particular, for any
m\geq 3
, the investigation of the generating function of
m
-regular maps endowed with a spin configuration reduces to studying the generating function of bipartite maps with vertex degrees 2 and
m
, rooted at a vertex of degree
m
. Section
5.2
focuses on this analysis in the quartic case, i.e., when
m=4
.

Remark 4.4
.

The change of variables
\Theta
is invertible on
\mathbb{Q}\llbracket\underline{x},\underline{y},t,\nu,u\rrbracket
, indeed we have:

\Theta^{-1}\Big(A\big(\underline{x},\underline{y},t,\nu,u\big)\Big)=A\Big(\underline{x},\underline{y},t\,(1-\nu^{2}),\nu,u\Big),

so that we can write
\Theta^{-1}(M_{\circ}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{262144}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\hbox to5.92pt{\vbox to5.92pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.39833pt\lower-0.39833pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{
{}{{}}{}

.
However, this change of variables
\Theta^{-1}
is not combinatorial. By that we mean that, if
A\in\mathbb{Q}\llbracket\underline{x},\underline{y},\nu,u\rrbracket
has non-negative coefficients, then
\Theta^{-1}(A)
does not necessarily have non-negative coefficients.

However, in the next section, we will prove that
\Theta^{-1}(B_{1})
does have non-negative coefficients.

4.3.
Combinatorial interpretation of well-charged trees of charge 1.

In this section, we give an interpretation of the formal power series
\Theta^{-1}\left(B_{1}(u)\right)
, as the generating function of a family of maps endowed with a spin configuration.

Let
\mathcal{R}
be the set of quadruples
(\mathrm{m},\rho,\tau,\sigma)
consisting of a rooted planar map endowed with a spin configuration
\sigma
, with a white root vertex
\rho
of degree one and with a marked black vertex
\tau
also of degree one. Since
\rho
and
\tau
have degree one, we identify them with their unique incident corners. Moreover, we do not require them to be incident to the same face, see Figure
14
.

Let
R(\underline{x},\underline{y},t,\nu,u)\in\mathbb{Q}\llbracket\underline{x},\underline{y},t,\nu,u\rrbracket
be the weighted generating function of maps in
\mathcal{R}
, defined as follows:

R(\underline{x},\underline{y},t,\nu,u)\coloneqq\sum\limits_{(\mathrm{m},\tau,\sigma)\in\mathcal{R}}{{(x_{1}y_{1})}^{-1}\,w^{\mathrm{Ising}}(\mathrm{m},\sigma)},

where
w^{\mathrm{Ising}}
is defined in (
4.1
). Note that the factor
{1}/{x_{1}y_{1}}
cancels the weights of the root vertex and of the marked vertex. We have the following:

(\mathrm{m}_{3},\rho_{3},\tau_{3})\in\mathcal{P}
(right).

Proof.

We begin by interpreting the power series
P(\underline{x},\underline{y},u)
as the generating function of the following set of
bipartite
maps, see Figure
14
.
Let
\mathcal{P}
denote the set of rooted
bipartite
maps with:

(1)

a white root vertex
\rho
of degree one, and

(2)

a distinguished black vertex
\tau
of degree one.

Equivalently,
\mathcal{P}
is the set of
\mathsf{d}
-trumpets rooted at a white vertex of degree one and a black marked vertex of degree one. It follows from Theorem
2.18
, that it is in bijection with the set of well-charged trees of charge
1
, rooted at a white vertex of degree 1. Excluding the tree with a single white vertex incident to a closing stem, these trees are in bijection with black well-charged planted trees of charge 1.
Hence, the generating series of
\mathcal{P}
(with no weight given to
\tau
and
\rho
) is equal to the formal power series
P(\underline{x},\underline{y},u)
.
Equivalently, we have:

P(\underline{x},\underline{y},u)=\sum\limits_{\mathrm{m}\in\mathcal{P}}{{(x_{1}y_{1})}^{-1}\,w(\mathrm{m})}.

\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}$}}}}}}
and the maps in
\mathcal{R}
. In particular, Equation (
4.6
) is now just a specialization of Claim
4.3
.
∎

5.
Explicit computations in the quartic case

In this section, we apply the previous enumerative results to quartic maps, i.e. maps for which every vertex has degree
4
. We first provide explicit parametrizations for the generating functions of bipartite maps of degree
2
and
4
, then derive explicit expressions for quartic maps with an Ising model. All computations performed in this section are detailed in the Maple companion
[
3
]
.

5.1.
Bipartite maps with vertex degrees
2
and
4

In this section, we set
x_{k}=y_{k}=0
for all
k\notin\left\{2,4\right\}
, then we have:

Proposition 5.1
.

Let
P
be the unique formal power series in
\mathbb{Q}\left[u\right]\llbracket x_{2},x_{4},y_{2},y_{4}\rrbracket
with constant term
u
satisfying

P=u+3x_{4}y_{4}P^{3}+P\frac{\big(x_{2}+3x_{4}y_{2}P\big)\big(y_{2}+3x_{2}y_{4}P\big)}{\big(1-9x_{4}y_{4}P^{2}\big)^{2}}.

(5.1)

The generating function of white-rooted bipartite planar maps
M_{\circ}(u)
can be expressed in terms of
P
as follows:

M_{\circ}(u)=15(x_{4}y_{4})^{2}P^{6}-5x_{4}y_{4}P^{4}+4ux_{4}y_{4}P^{3}+\frac{1}{3}(x_{2}y_{2}-1)\,P^{2}+\frac{4}{3}uP-u^{2}.

(5.2)

Proof.

We first compute the generating series of well-charged trees.
Note that, due to the vertex degree constraint, the only charges allowed are equal to
1
or
3
for white vertices, and to
-3,-1
or
1
for black vertices. The recursive equations (
3.4
) and (
3.5
) can be rewritten as the following system of equations, obtained in
[
16
, Sec. 6.1]
:

\centering\begin{cases}B_{-3}(u)&\hskip-7.39755pt=y_{4}u^{3},\\
B_{-1}(u)&\hskip-7.39755pt=y_{2}u+3y_{4}u^{2}\,W_{1}(u),\\
B_{1}(u)&\hskip-7.39755pt=y_{2}\,W_{1}(u)+3y_{4}\,\Big(u^{2}W_{3}(u)+uW_{1}(u)^{2}\Big),\\
W_{1}(u)&\hskip-7.39755pt=x_{2}\,\Big(1+B_{1}(u)\Big)+3x_{4}\,B_{-1}(u)\,\Big(1+B_{1}(u)\Big)^{2},\\
W_{3}(u)&\hskip-7.39755pt=x_{4}\,\Big(1+B_{1}(u)\Big)^{3}.\end{cases}\@add@centering

As already established in
[
16
, Eq.(7) and (8)]
, the formal power series
P:=P(\underline{x},\underline{y},\nu,u)=u\,(1+B_{1}(u))
is the unique formal power series with constant coefficient equal to
u
and satisfying (
5.1
). Moreover, all the generating series of well-charged trees can be expressed in terms of
P
, and we have:

B_{-3}(u)=y_{4}u^{3},\qquad B_{-1}(u)=u\frac{y_{2}+3x_{2}y_{4}P}{1-9x_{4}y_{4}P^{2}},\qquad B_{1}(u)=\frac{P}{u}-1,

and

W_{1}(u)=\frac{P(x_{2}+3y_{2}x_{4}P)}{u(1-9x_{4}y_{4}P^{2})},\qquad W_{3}(u)=x_{4}\frac{P^{3}}{u^{3}}.

From now on, our approach differs from the one in
[
16
]
: we apply Theorem
2.5
to express the generating series
\bar{M}_{\circ}(u)
of plane rooted bipartite maps in terms of the generating series of blossoming trees (instead of considering
balanced
blossoming trees). Applying Proposition
3.1
, we get:

\bar{M}_{\circ}(u)=2x_{2}P\frac{y_{2}+3x_{2}y_{4}P}{1-9x_{4}y_{4}P^{2}}+2x_{4}P^{2}\left(y_{4}P+3\left(\frac{y_{2}+3x_{2}y_{4}P}{1-9x_{4}y_{4}P^{2}}\right)^{2}\right).

(5.3)

By (
3.8
) and since
M_{\circ}(0)=0
, the series
M_{\circ}(u)
can then be expressed as the following formal integral:

M_{\circ}(u)=\int_{0}^{u}\bar{M}_{\circ}(v)dv=\int_{0}^{u}\frac{\bar{M}_{\circ}(\underline{x},\underline{y},v)}{\partial_{v}P(\underline{x},\underline{y},\nu,v)}\partial_{v}P(\underline{x},\underline{y},v)dv.

(5.4)

Next, we prove that

\frac{\bar{M}_{\circ}(\underline{x},\underline{y},u)}{\partial_{u}P(\underline{x},\underline{y},u)}=\mathrm{Rat}(\underline{x},\underline{y},P(\underline{x},\underline{y},u)),

(5.5)

where
\mathrm{Rat}
is a rational function. Indeed, from (
5.3
), we know that
\bar{M}_{\circ}
is a rational function in
\underline{x}
,
\underline{y}
and
P
. Then, differentiating (
5.1
) with respect to
u
, we get that there exist two polynomials
Q_{1},Q_{2}\in\mathbb{Q}[\underline{x},\underline{y},p]
such that:

\partial_{u}P(\underline{x},\underline{y},u)\cdot Q_{1}(\underline{x},\underline{y},P(\underline{x},\underline{y},u))=Q_{2}(\underline{x},\underline{y},P(\underline{x},\underline{y},u)),

and (
5.5
) follows.

By an elementary change of variables, and using that
P(\underline{x},\underline{y},0)=0
, we obtain:

M_{\circ}(u)=\int_{0}^{u}\mathrm{Rat}(\underline{x},\underline{y},P(\underline{x},\underline{y},v))\partial_{v}P(\underline{x},\underline{y},v)dv=\int_{0}^{P(\underline{x},\underline{y},u)}\mathrm{Rat}(p)dp.

The integral can be computed explicitly, and it turns out that it still belongs to
\mathbb{Q}(x_{2},x_{4},y_{2},y_{4},P(u))
, see the Maple companion file
[
3
]
. We can further simplify this expression using (
5.1
), which concludes the proof.
∎

Following exactly the same chain of arguments, we can specialize this result to maps rooted at a vertex of degree 4. The only difference is to replace (
5.3
) by the following appropriate expression for the generating series
\bar{M}_{\circ,4}
of bipartite plane maps rooted on a white vertex of degree 4:

\bar{M}_{\circ,4}(u)=2x_{4}P^{2}\left(y_{4}P+3\left(\frac{y_{2}+3x_{2}y_{4}P}{1-9x_{4}y_{4}P^{2}}\right)^{2}\right).

(5.6)

We obtain:

Proposition 5.2

(see also Proposition 22 of
[
16
]
)
.

The generating function of rooted bipartite planar maps with a white root vertex of degree 4 – denoted by
M_{\circ,4}
– can be expressed in terms of
P
as follows:

M_{\circ,4}(x_{2},x_{4},y_{2},y_{4},u)=\frac{\mathrm{Pol}(x_{2},x_{4},y_{2},y_{4},u,P)}{9x_{4}\left(9P^{2}x_{4}y_{4}-1\right)}.

(5.7)

where
\mathrm{Pol}(x_{2},x_{4},y_{2},y_{4},u,p)\in\mathbb{Q}\left[x_{2},x_{4},y_{2},y_{4},u,p\right]
is defined as follows:

\displaystyle\mathrm{Pol}(x_{2},x_{4},y_{2},y_{4},u,p)

\displaystyle\quad\coloneqq 1215{x_{4}}^{4}{y_{4}}^{3}p^{8}-540{x_{4}}^{3}{y_{4}}^{2}p^{6}+27{x_{4}}^{2}{y_{4}}^{2}\left(12x_{4}u-{x_{2}}^{2}\right)p^{5}+18{x_{4}}^{2}y_{4}\left(1-x_{2}y_{2}\right)p^{4}

\displaystyle\qquad+6x_{4}y_{4}\left(12x_{4}u-5{x_{2}}^{2}\right)p^{3}+\left(45{x_{2}}^{2}x_{4}y_{4}u+3x_{4}-3{x_{2}}^{4}y_{4}-6x_{4}x_{2}y_{2}-81x_{4}^{2}y_{4}u^{2}\right)p^{2}

\displaystyle\qquad+\left(1-x_{2}y_{2}\right)\left({x_{2}}^{2}-12x_{4}u\right)p+u\left(9x_{4}u-{x_{2}}^{2}\right).

Note that this parametrization differs from the one in
[
16
]
, which was obtained using a different “unpointing”. Still both are equivalent, and one can be derived from the other by using (
5.1
).

5.2.
The Ising model in the quartic case

In this section, we deal with white-rooted quartic planar maps endowed with a spin configuration, i.e. we set
x_{k}=y_{k}=0
for all
k\neq 4
. To simplify notation, we write
x=x_{4}
,
y=y_{4}
and
I_{\circ}(x,y,t,\nu,u)=I_{\circ}(\underline{x},\underline{y},t,\nu,u)
with a slight abuse of notation.
We establish the following explicit Lagrangian rational parametrization for
I_{\circ}(u)
in terms of a new formal power series with
non-negative
coefficients:

Theorem 5.3
.

The generating function of white-rooted quartic planar maps endowed with a spin configuration is given by:

I_{\circ}(x,y,t,\nu,u)=\frac{\mathrm{Pol}_{I_{\circ}}(x,y,t,\nu,u,Q)}{9\left(1-\nu^{2}\right)t^{4}\Big(1+3x\left(1-\nu^{2}\right)Q\Big)},

(5.8)

where the series
Q\equiv Q(x,y,t,\nu,u)
is the unique formal power series in
\mathbb{Q}(x,y,\nu,u)\llbracket t\rrbracket
with constant term 0 that satisfies the Lagrangian equation:

t^{2}=\frac{Q\left(1-3\nu^{2}\left(x+y\right)Q-3xy\left(1-\nu^{2}\right)\left(3\nu^{2}+7\right)Q^{2}+135x^{2}y^{2}\left(1-\nu^{2}\right)^{3}Q^{4}-243x^{3}y^{3}\left(1-\nu^{2}\right)^{5}Q^{6}\right)}{u\,{\left(1-9xy\left(1-\nu^{2}\right)^{2}Q^{2}\right)}^{2}},

(5.9)

and where the polynomial
\mathrm{Pol}_{I_{\circ}}(x,y,t,\nu,u,q)\in\mathbb{Q}\left[x,y,t,\nu,u,q\right]
is defined as follows:

\displaystyle\mathrm{Pol}_{I_{\circ}}(x,y,t,\nu,u,q)

\displaystyle\hskip 8.19447pt\coloneqq 405x^{3}y^{2}\left(1-\nu^{2}\right)^{4}q^{7}+351x^{2}y^{2}\left(1-\nu^{2}\right)^{3}q^{6}+27xy\left(1-\nu^{2}\right)^{2}\left(\nu^{2}y-\left(5+12t^{2}u\left(1-\nu^{2}\right)^{2}y\right)x\right)q^{5}

\displaystyle\hskip 8.19447pt+3xy\left(1-\nu^{2}\right)\left(36t^{2}u\left(1-\nu^{2}\right)^{2}x-3\nu^{2}-47\right)q^{4}+\left(\left(252t^{2}u\left(1-\nu^{2}\right)^{2}y-6\nu^{2}-9\right)x-15\nu^{2}y\right)q^{3}

\displaystyle\hskip 8.19447pt+\left(\left(36t^{2}u\left(1-\nu^{2}\right)-108t^{4}u^{2}y\left(1-\nu^{2}\right)^{3}\right)x+5+9\nu^{2}t^{2}u\left(1-\nu^{2}\right)y\right)q^{2}-t^{2}u\left(27t^{2}u\left(1-\nu^{2}\right)^{2}x-3\nu^{2}+8\right)q

\displaystyle\hskip 8.19447pt+3t^{4}u^{2}\left(1-\nu^{2}\right).

Moreover, the series
Q
has non-negative integer coefficients.

Proof.

All the computations performed for this proof are available in the Maple companion file
[
3
]
.
We first observe that, in this setting,
M_{\circ}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{262144}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\hbox to5.92pt{\vbox to5.92pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.39833pt\lower-0.39833pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{

\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}

\lxSVG@closescope }

. By Claim

The fact that
Q
satisfies Equation (
5.9
) follows directly from applying the change of variables
\Theta^{-1}
in the equation verified by
P
given in Proposition
5.1
. Finally Proposition
4.5
establishes that

Q=t\,R(\underline{x},\underline{y},t,\nu,u),

with the specialization
x_{k}=y_{k}=0
for all
k\neq 4
. Thus,
Q
is also the generating function of a family of Ising maps, and its coefficients are non-negative integers.
∎

Appendix A
Geodesic properties of
\alpha_{d}
-orientations and connection with Eulerian orientations

In this appendix, we further explore the properties of
\alpha_{d}
-orientations on bipartite maps. We begin by examining their connection to the dual directed geodesic labeling, and then we show how they generalize the well-known Eulerian and quasi-Eulerian orientations.

A.1.
Geodesic properties of
\alpha_{d}
-orientations

A.1.1.
Directed geodesic labeling

A
directed
planar map is a planar map in which every edge has a direction, in other words it is the planar embedding of a
directed
graph. Let
\mathrm{m}
be a directed map that is strongly connected, meaning that there exists a directed path between every pair of vertices. We can then define a
directed quasi-distance
on
\mathrm{m}
as follows: For two vertices
u,v\in\mathrm{V}(\mathrm{m})
, the directed distance – denoted by
\vec{d}(u,v)
– from
u
to
v
is defined as the length of a shortest directed path from
u
to
v
.

Additionally if
\mathrm{m}
is pointed at
v_{\star}
, then the
(directed) geodesic labeling

\ell:\mathrm{V}(\mathrm{m})\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
is defined by
\ell(v)=\vec{d}(v_{\star},v)
for any
v\in\mathrm{V}(\mathrm{m})
. The geodesic labeling also admits the following characterization:

Proposition A.1
.

Let
(\mathrm{m},v_{\star})
be a pointed directed planar map. Consider a labeling of its vertices
{\ell:\mathrm{V}(\mathrm{m})\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}}
, such that:

(1)

\ell(v_{\star})=0
,

(2)

for any
v\in\mathrm{V}(\mathrm{m})\backslash\{v_{\star}\}
, there exists a directed edge from a vertex
u
to
v
in
\mathrm{m}
such that
{\ell(v)=\ell(u)+1}
,

(3)

for any directed edge from
u
to
v
in
\mathrm{m}
,
\ell(v)\leq\ell(u)+1
.

Then
\ell
is the directed geodesic labeling of
\mathrm{m}
.

Proof.

First, the geodesic labeling on
\mathrm{m}
clearly satisfies conditions 1, 2 and 3. Reciprocally,
2.
and
3.
together imply that, for any
v\in\mathrm{V}(\mathrm{m})\backslash\{v_{\star}\}
, we have:

\ell(v)=\min\{\ell(u)+1,\text{ where }u\text{ is such that }\overrightarrow{uv}\in\mathrm{m}\}.

Then, the conclusion follows by an immediate induction on the value of
\ell(v)
.
∎

A.1.2.
Geodesic properties of
\alpha_{d}
-orientations

In this section, we prove that the minimal
\alpha_{d}
-orientations of a bipartite map are deeply related to the geodesic labeling on its dual (Eulerian) map, see Figure
15
for an illustration.

Proposition A.2
.

Let
\mathrm{m}
be a rooted bipartite plane map.
Fix
d\geq\Delta_{\circ}(\mathrm{m})
and let
\mathcal{O}
be its minimal
\alpha_{d}
-orientation.

Let
(\mathrm{m}^{\dagger},v_{\star}^{\dagger})
be the dual map of
\mathrm{m}
, which is pointed at the vertex
v_{\star}^{\dagger}
corresponding to the outer face of
\mathrm{m}
. Recall from Section
1.1.2
that the edges of
\mathrm{m}^{\dagger}
are canonically oriented by requiring that white faces are on their left, and write
\ell:\mathrm{V}(\mathrm{m}^{\dagger})\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
for the directed geodesic labeling on
(\mathrm{m}^{\dagger},v_{\star}^{\dagger})
.

Then, for any directed edge
e^{\dagger}=(v_{1}^{\dagger},v_{2}^{\dagger})\in\mathrm{E}(\mathrm{m}^{\dagger})
, we have:

\ell(v_{1}^{\dagger})-\ell(v_{2}^{\dagger})=\mathcal{O}_{\circ}(e)-1,

(A.1)

where
e\in\mathrm{E}(\mathrm{m})
is the dual edge of
e^{\dagger}
, and where
\mathcal{O}_{\circ}(e)
stands for the orientation of the half-edge of
e
incident to its white endpoint.

Figure 15
.
Correspondence between the minimal
\alpha_{d}
orientation and the dual geodesic labeling (left), and an example of a rooted bipartite plane map - drawn in black - and of its dual rooted pointed Eulerian map - drawn in blue (right).

Remark A.3
.

This proposition is closely related to
[
11
, Lemma 18]
, where the authors introduced a family of orientations on Eulerian maps (called hypermaps), and prove a characterization of these orientations similar to Proposition
A.1
, but in the dual setting.

Remark A.4
.

Note that Proposition
A.1
gives another proof of Property
2.4
. In fact, for minimal orientations, the value of the orientation of half-edges incident to white vertices does not depend on
d
, as long as
d\geq\Delta_{\circ}
; and the value of the orientation of half-edges incident to black vertices can be deduced immediately.

Proof of Proposition
A.2
.

The proof of the proposition proceeds in two steps. First, we prove that the conditions given in (
A.1
) are consistent; i.e. that there exists a labeling
\tilde{\ell}
that verifies all of them (and such that
\tilde{\ell}(v_{\star}^{\dagger})=0
). Second, we show that
\tilde{\ell}
satisfies all the conditions of Proposition
A.1
, and thus coincides with the dual geodesic labeling.

To address the first point, fix a spanning tree
\mathrm{t}
of
\mathrm{m}^{\dagger}
. Set
\tilde{\ell}(v_{\star}^{\dagger})=0
, and extend the labeling to each vertex
v^{\dagger}\in\mathrm{V}(\mathrm{m}^{\dagger})
by successively applying Equation (
A.1
) along the path from the pointed vertex
v_{\star}^{\dagger}
to
v^{\dagger}
in the spanning tree
\mathrm{t}
. We can prove that:

Lemma A.5
.

The labeling
\tilde{\ell}
obtained does not depend on the choice of
\mathrm{t}
.

The proof of the lemma is postponed to the end of the proof of the Proposition. Finally, we prove that
\tilde{\ell}
is the geodesic labeling of
\mathrm{m}^{\dagger}
, by applying the characterization of Proposition
A.1
. The first condition holds immediately by construction. To verify the second and third conditions, let
e^{\dagger}:=(v_{1}^{\dagger},v_{2}^{\dagger})\in E(\mathrm{m}^{\dagger})
as in the proposition. Since
\mathcal{O}_{\circ}(e)\geq 0
, we have:

\tilde{\ell}(v_{2}^{\dagger})=\tilde{\ell}(v_{1}^{\dagger})-\Big(\mathcal{O}_{\circ}(e)-1\Big)\leq\tilde{\ell}(v_{1}^{\dagger})+1.

(A.2)

This completes the verification of the third condition. To prove the second condition, observe that the inequality in (
A.2
) is an equality if and only if
e
is saturated from its black endpoint to its white endpoint. Let
f_{2}
be the face of
\mathrm{m}
dual to
v_{2}^{\dagger}
. Since
\mathcal{O}
is minimal, there exists a saturated edge
\tilde{e}\in\mathrm{E}(\mathrm{m})
incident to
f_{2}
, and such that
f_{2}
lies on its right. By Property
2.3
,
\tilde{e}
is saturated from its black endpoint to its white endpoint. This concludes the proof.
∎

(a)

Counterclockwise cycle surrounding several faces, and

(b)

directed contours of a white and black face, respectively.

Figure 16
.
A general cycle where
\varepsilon(\overrightarrow{e_{1}^{\dagger}})=1
and
\varepsilon(\overrightarrow{e_{i}^{\dagger}})=-1
, with the dashed area indicating the interior of
\mathscr{C}
(left), and cycles being the direct contour of white and black face, respectively, where
\varepsilon(\overrightarrow{e_{i}^{\dagger}})
is constantly
1
(right).

We end this section by proving Lemma
A.5
.

Proof of Lemma
A.5
.

The proof is illustrated in Figure
16
. Let
\overrightarrow{e^{\dagger}}
be a directed edge of
\mathrm{m}^{\dagger}
. We set:

\varepsilon(\overrightarrow{e^{\dagger}})\coloneqq\begin{cases}+1&if the direction of $\overrightarrow{e^{\dagger}}$ coincides with the canonical direction of $e^{\dagger}$ on $\mathrm{m}^{\dagger}$\\
-1&otherwise.\end{cases}

Let
\mathscr{C}:=(\overrightarrow{e_{1}^{\dagger}},\ldots,\overrightarrow{e_{k}^{\dagger}})
be a simple directed cycle in
\mathrm{m}^{\dagger}
. To prove the lemma, it suffices to show that the total variation of the labels along
\mathscr{C}
, as defined by (
A.1
), is equal to zero; or, more formally that:

\sum_{i=1}^{k}\varepsilon(\overrightarrow{e_{i}^{\dagger}})\,\big(\mathcal{O}_{\circ}(e_{i})-1\big)=0,

(A.3)

where
e_{i}
stands for the dual edge of
e_{i}^{\dagger}
for any
i\in\left\{1,\ldots,k\right\}
.

The cycle
\mathscr{C}
disconnects
\mathrm{m}^{\dagger}
into 2 connected components composed of faces. The
interior
of
\mathscr{C}
is defined as the one which does not contain
v_{\star}^{\dagger}
. Since the contour of
\mathscr{C}
can be written as the sum of the contours of the faces in its interior, it is in fact enough to establish (
A.3
) when
\mathscr{C}
is the contour of a face.

Assume that
\mathscr{C}
is the directed contour of a white face of
\mathrm{m}^{\dagger}
, so that
\varepsilon(\overrightarrow{e_{i}^{\dagger}})=1
, for any
1\leq i\leq k
. Write
v_{\circ}
for the vertex of
\mathrm{m}
associated with this face.
By definition of
\alpha_{d}
-orientations, we have:

\sum_{i=1}^{k}\Big(\mathcal{O}_{\circ}(e_{i})-1\Big)=\sum_{h\sim v_{\circ}}{\mathcal{O}(h)}-\deg(v_{\circ})=\alpha_{d}(v_{\circ})-\deg(v_{\circ})=0.

The case of a black face is similar and is left to the reader.
∎

A.2.
Connection between
\alpha_{d}
-orientations and Eulerian orientations

In this section, we show how
\alpha_{d}
-orientations generalize the classical notions of Eulerian (or more precisely quasi-Eulerian) orientations to bipartite maps. Unlike in the rest of the article, here,
\mathrm{m}
refers to a general map, which is not necessarily bipartite.

An
Eulerian orientation
is a
1
-fractional
\alpha
-orientation, where
{\alpha(\cdot)\coloneqq\deg(\cdot)}/2
.
If
\mathrm{m}
is Eulerian, i.e. if all its vertices have even degree, it is classical and follows directly from Euler’s theorem, that
\mathrm{m}
can be endowed with an Eulerian orientation, and that any Eulerian orientation is accessible. More generally, a
quasi-Eulerian orientation
is a
2
-fractional
\alpha
-orientation with
{\alpha(\cdot)\coloneqq\deg(\cdot)}
. It is straightforward to see that any planar map can be endowed with a quasi-Eulerian orientation, and that any of them is accessible. Moreover, by Proposition
1.1
, every Eulerian planar map admits a unique minimal accessible Eulerian orientation, and every planar map admits a unique minimal accessible quasi-Eulerian orientation.

Figure 17
.
Quasi-Eulerian orientations. A plane map with its minimal quasi-Eulerian orientation and dual undirected geodesic labeling (left), the possible orientations of an edge, its connection to the dual labeling, and the corresponding hypermap with black faces of degree 2 (right).

Minimal Eulerian and quasi-Eulerian orientations are known to be deeply related to the geodesic labeling on
\mathrm{m}^{\dagger}
, where the geodesic labeling is defined here for the classical notion of graph distance on
\mathrm{m}^{\dagger}
, and not on its directed definition as in Section
A.1.1
. More precisely, as illustrated on Figure
17
, in the minimal orientation:

•

non-saturated edges separate faces with equal geodesic labeling,

•

saturated edges separate faces with different geodesic labeling, such that the face with higher label is on the right of the saturated edge. Note that, in that case, both labels differ exactly by 1, since we consider the non-directed geodesic labeling.

This fact can be recovered as a special case of Proposition
A.2
. Indeed, any planar map can be transformed into a bipartite map by inserting a black vertex (of degree 2) in the middle of each of its edges. Dually, this corresponds to the usual transformation of planar maps into hypermaps by replacing every edge by a face of degree 2. Observe that the directed geodesic labeling on the hypermap obtained coincides with the undirected geodesic labeling on the initial map.

Let
\mathrm{m}
be a rooted planar map, and let
\tilde{\mathrm{m}}
be its associated rooted planar bipartite map. Write
\Delta
for the maximal vertex degree of
\mathrm{m}
. Note that
\Delta=\Delta_{\circ}(\tilde{\mathrm{m}})
. There is a natural one-to-one correspondence between quasi-Eulerian orientations of
\mathrm{m}
and
\alpha_{\Delta}
-orientations of
\tilde{\mathrm{m}}
. Precisely, given a quasi-Eulerian orientation
\mathcal{O}
of
\mathrm{m}
, the associated
\alpha_{\Delta}
-orientation
\tilde{\mathcal{O}}
of
\tilde{\mathrm{m}}
is defined as follows. For any edge
\tilde{e}=\{h_{\circ},h_{\bullet}\}\in\mathrm{E}(\tilde{\mathrm{m}})
:

\begin{cases}\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(h_{\circ})\coloneqq\mathcal{O}(h_{\circ}),\\
\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(h_{\bullet})\coloneqq(\Delta+1)-\mathcal{O}(h_{\circ}).\end{cases}

Accessibility and minimality are preserved. Thus, as a consequence of Proposition
A.2
, we recover as a special case, the correspondence between the minimal quasi-Eulerian orientation of a plane map and the geodesic labeling of its dual map.

Appendix B
Recovering mobiles and BDG bijection with
\alpha_{d}
-orientations

In this section, we apply to the class of
\alpha_{d}
-orientations, the generic bijective scheme introduced by Bernardi and Fusy in
[
8
]
, and further expanded in
[
9
,
10
,
11
]
.
This allows us to recover the well-known bijection between Eulerian planar maps and “mobiles”, due to Bouttier, Di Francesco and Guitter
[
19
]
. As already mentioned in the introduction, the “mobile” bijection was already recovered as a special case of a generic framework developed for hypermaps by Bernardi and Fusy
[
11
]
. The novelty of our result is that we apply here their “classical” framework, i.e. the one they developed for classical maps in
[
8
]
and not for hypermaps.

In Bernardi–Fusy’s bijection and in Bouttier–Di Francesco–Guitter’s bijection, the central objects are decorated trees that are referred to as
mobiles
. However, the definition of mobiles differ between the two articles
[
8
,
19
]
. To avoid confusion, we will refer to the mobiles in Bernardi–Fusy’s bijection as
blossoming mobiles
(since they carry opening and closing stems), and the mobiles in Bouttier–Di Francesco–Guitter’s bijection as
labeled mobiles
(since they are labeled!).

In contrast to the rest of the article, this appendix concerns exclusively
unrooted
bipartite plane maps.

B.1.
Bernardi-Fusy’s bijective scheme.

Following
[
8
]
, a
blossoming mobile
is an unrooted plane blossoming tree
\mathrm{t}
endowed with an orientation
\mathcal{O}
, and which satisfies the following properties:

•

the vertices of the tree can be of 2 types: either round or square,

•

the dangling half-edges are opening stems (i.e. outgoing) and are incident to square vertices, and

•

for any half-edge
h\in\mathrm{H}(\mathrm{t})
,
\mathcal{O}(h)=0
if and only if
h
is an outgoing stem or if
h
is part of a round-square edge and is incident to the round vertex. Otherwise, we require that
\mathcal{O}(h)>0
.

The
excess
of a blossoming mobile is the number of its half-edges adjacent to a round vertex minus the number of its opening stems. Note that it differs from the notion of excess for blossoming trees defined in Section
2.2.3
. Finally, the
weight
of an edge in a blossoming mobile is the sum of the orientation of its two half-edges.

The generic bijective scheme of
[
8
]
is a bijective operation
\Phi_{\mathrm{BF}}
that associates to a blossoming map
\mathrm{m}
endowed with an accessible and minimal orientation
\mathcal{O}
, a blossoming mobile.

Remark B.1
.

Note that the maps considered here are unrooted, so the notion of accessibility needs to be adapted. In this appendix, following
[
8
]
we say that an orientation on a plane map is
accessible
if there exists an outer vertex that is accessible from every other vertex.

Let us recall the definition of
\Phi_{\mathrm{BF}}(m,\mathcal{O})
: First, insert a square vertex into each face of
\mathrm{m}
, and then apply the following
local transformation
to each edge of
\mathrm{m}
, see Figure
18
:

•

The
local transformation
of a non-saturated edge
e\in\mathrm{E}(\mathrm{m})
consists in keeping it and adding an opening stem, pointing towards
e
, on each of the two square vertices placed in its two adjacent faces. The orientation of
e
is preserved.

•

The
local transformation
of a saturated edge
e\in\mathrm{E}(\mathrm{m})
from a vertex
u
to a vertex
v
, consists in adding an opening stem, pointing towards
e
, on the square vertex in its left face, then creating an edge between the square vertex in its right face and
v
, and removing
e
. The created square-round edge inherits the orientation of
e
, meaning that it is saturated from the square vertex to
v
.

Then, one of the main results of
[
8
]
is:

Figure 18
.
The local transformation of an edge following Bernardi-Fusy’s bijection: the non-saturated case (left), and the saturated case (right).

Theorem B.2

(
[
8
]
, Theorem 11)
.

The mapping
\Phi_{\mathrm{BF}}
is a bijection between the set of unrooted plane maps endowed with a minimal accessible orientation and the set of blossoming mobiles of positive excess.

Remark B.3
.

The bijective mapping
\Phi_{\mathrm{BF}}
induces several parameter correspondences. Specifically, let
\mathrm{m}
be a plane map endowed with a minimal accessible orientation
\mathcal{O}
, and let
\mathrm{t}=\Phi_{\mathrm{BF}}(\mathrm{m},\mathcal{O})
. There are correspondences between :

•

the degrees of the outer face of
\mathrm{m}
and the excess of
\mathrm{t}
,

•

the degrees of the other faces of
\mathrm{m}
and the degrees of the square vertices of
\mathrm{t}
,

•

the indegrees of the vertices of
\mathrm{m}
and the indegrees of the round vertices of
\mathrm{t}
,

•

the saturated edges of
\mathrm{m}
and the round-square edges in
\mathrm{t}
.

Moreover, if
\mathcal{O}
is
k
-fractional, then every edge of
\mathrm{t}
has weight
k
.

Remark B.4
.

The previous theorem is a specific case of the more general bijective scheme defined by Bernardi and Fusy in
[
8
]
, tailored to fit our presentation. For the sake of simplicity and consistency with the rest of the present article, we have also slightly adapted their conventions for defining the transformation of maps.

B.2.
Application to
\alpha_{d}
-orientations

To apply Theorem
B.2
to the context of bipartite maps, we need to introduce the following family of blossoming mobiles.
Fix
d\geq 1
. A
d
-blossoming mobile
is a blossoming mobile
\mathrm{t}
, where round vertices are additionally colored in black and white and which additionally satisfies the following:

(1)

it has positive excess,

(2)

all edges are either between a white and a black vertices or between a white vertex and a square vertex,

(3)

with maximum white vertex degree
d
,

and endowed with a
(d+1)
-fractional orientation
\mathcal{O}:\mathrm{H}(\mathrm{t})\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
such that

(4)

every white vertex
v
satisfies
\mathrm{deg_{in}}(v)=d\cdot\deg(v)
,

(5)

every black vertex
v
verifies
\mathrm{deg_{in}}(v)=\mathrm{d}(v)
, where
\mathrm{d}(v)
is the degree of
v
in the oriented map
{\Phi_{\mathrm{BF}}}^{-1}(\mathrm{t})
, and

(6)

without saturated white-black edges.

Then Theorem
B.2
can be specialized to bipartite maps endowed with their minimal
\alpha_{d}
-orientation, as follows, see Figure
20
for an example.

Corollary B.5
.

Fix
d\geq 1
. The mapping
\Phi_{\mathrm{BF}}
induces a bijection between the set of bipartite plane maps with maximum white vertex degree
d
and endowed with their minimal
\alpha_{d}
-orientation, and the set of
d
-blossoming mobiles.

Proof.

Let
\mathrm{m}
be a bipartite plane map, with maximum white vertex degree
d
, and endowed with its minimal
\alpha_{d}
-orientation
\mathcal{O}
, and let
\mathrm{t}=\Phi_{\mathrm{BF}}(\mathrm{m},\mathcal{O})
. We start by proving that
\mathrm{t}
is a
d
-blossoming mobile.

First, as a direct consequence of Theorem
B.2
,
\mathrm{t}
has positive excess so that (1) holds.

Then, every edge
e
of
\mathrm{t}
either corresponds to the local transformation of a non-saturated edge in
(\mathrm{m},\mathcal{O})
, in which case it connects a black vertex and a white vertex, or it corresponds to a saturated edge
\tilde{e}
in
(\mathrm{m},\mathcal{O})
. In the latter case, Property
2.3
ensures that
\tilde{e}
is saturated from its black endpoint to its white endpoint. Then, the local transformation imply that
e
is a white-square edge, see Figure
18
. Properties (2) and (6) then follow.

Finally, it is straightforward that
\mathrm{deg_{in}}(v)=d\cdot\deg_{\mathrm{m}}(v)
for any
v\in V_{\circ}(\mathrm{m})
, and
\mathrm{deg_{in}}(v)=\deg_{\mathrm{m}}(v)
for any
v\in V_{\bullet}(\mathrm{m})
, so that Properties (4) and (5) follow.

Reciprocally, let
\mathrm{t}
be a
d
-blossoming tree. It has positive excess by (1), allowing us to define
(\mathrm{m},\mathcal{O})={\Phi_{\mathrm{BF}}}^{-1}(\mathrm{t})
, as stated in Theorem
B.2
.

First, we prove that
\mathrm{m}
is bipartite. For any
{\oslash},\otimes\in\{\bullet,\circ,\text{\tiny{$\square$}}\}
, and
\mathrm{x}\in\{\mathrm{t},\mathrm{m}\}
, denote by
n_{\oslash\otimes}(\mathrm{x})
the number of edges in
\mathrm{x}
between two vertices of type
\oslash
and
\otimes
. Similarly, we denote respectively by
n^{\mathrm{sat}}_{\oslash\otimes}(\mathrm{x})
and
n^{\mathrm{nonsat}}_{\oslash\otimes}(\mathrm{x})
the number of saturated and non-saturated edges of this type. Finally, let
\mathrm{V}_{\oslash}(\mathrm{x})
denote the set of vertices of
\mathrm{x}
of type
\oslash
. With these notations in place, we aim to demonstrate that
n_{\bullet\bullet}(\mathrm{m})=n_{\circ\circ}(\mathrm{m})=0
.

By Property (2), every vertex adjacent to a square vertex in
\mathrm{t}
is both round and white, so that the edges of
\mathrm{m}
consist of the round-round edges of
\mathrm{t}
(which are also black-white) together with additional saturated edges connecting any colored vertex to a white vertex. Hence, one has:

n_{\bullet\bullet}(\mathrm{m})=0\quad\text{and}\quad n_{\circ\circ}(\mathrm{m})=n^{\mathrm{sat}}_{\circ\circ}(\mathrm{m}),

and from the correspondences induced by
\Phi_{\mathrm{BF}}
and (6), one has:

n_{\circ{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{262144}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\hbox to5.92pt{\vbox to5.92pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 2.1056pt\lower-2.1056pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{

(B.1)

Recall that in a blossoming mobile the orientation of every half-edge belonging to a round-square edge and being incident to the round vertex is zero, and that

. Therefore, it follows that:

\sum\limits_{v\in\mathrm{V}_{\circ}(\mathrm{t})}{\mathrm{deg_{out}}(v)}=\sum\limits_{v\in\mathrm{V}_{\bullet}(\mathrm{t})}{\mathrm{deg_{in}}(v)}.

(B.2)

Moreover, by Property (4) and (
B.1
), on the one hand, we have:

\sum\limits_{v\in\mathrm{V}_{\circ}(\mathrm{t})}{\mathrm{deg_{out}}(v)}=\sum\limits_{v\in\mathrm{V}_{\circ}(\mathrm{t})}{\deg(v)}=n_{\circ{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{262144}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\hbox to5.92pt{\vbox to5.92pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 2.1056pt\lower-2.1056pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{

\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{262144}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\hbox to5.92pt{\vbox to5.92pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 2.1056pt\lower-2.1056pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{

On the other hand, by Property (5), we have:

\sum\limits_{v\in\mathrm{V}_{\bullet}(\mathrm{t})}{\mathrm{deg_{in}}(v)}=\sum\limits_{v\in\mathrm{V}_{\bullet}(\mathrm{m})}{\deg(v)}=n^{\mathrm{sat}}_{\bullet\circ}(\mathrm{m})+n^{\mathrm{nonsat}}_{\bullet\circ}(\mathrm{m}).

We conclude that
n^{\mathrm{sat}}_{\circ\circ}(\mathrm{m})=0
. Thus,
\mathrm{m}
is bipartite.

Since the white vertex degree is preserved by
\Phi_{\mathrm{BF}}
, Property (3) ensures that
\mathrm{m}
has maximum white vertex degree
d
.
Lastly,
\mathcal{O}
is an
\alpha_{d}
-orientation on
\mathrm{m}
, which follows from the definition of
\Phi_{\mathrm{BF}}
and from Properties (4) and (5).
∎

B.3.
Recovering the Bouttier-Di Francesco-Guitter bijection for Eulerian maps

In this section, we show that the bijection presented in the previous section is, in fact, equivalent – up to minor adjustments of the trees – to the celebrated BDG bijection, introduced by Bouttier, Di Francesco, and Guitter in
[
19
]
. This is illustrated on Figure
20
.

B.3.1.
BDG bijection

Let us first recall the BDG bijection. We begin by introducing several definitions.
A
labeled mobile
is a plane tree, in which vertices can be of 3 types: either black and round, white and round or square, with the requirement that all edges are either of type white-black or white-square. Additionally, labels are assigned to square vertices and to each side of any white-black edge – the latter are referred to as
flags labels
– subject to the following conditions:

(1)

each flag label is non-negative, and at least one is zero,

(2)

each square vertex has a positive label,

(3)

in clockwise order around a black vertex, two consecutive labels
\ell_{1}
and
\ell_{2}
verify
\ell_{2}\leq\ell_{1}
if they are flag labels on the same edge, and
\ell_{2}\geq\ell_{1}
otherwise,

(4)

in clockwise order around a white vertex, two consecutive labels
\ell_{1}
and
\ell_{2}
verify
\ell_{2}\geq\ell_{1}
if they are flag labels on the same edge,
\ell_{2}=\ell_{1}-1
if
\ell_{1}
is a square vertex label, and
\ell_{2}=\ell_{1}
otherwise.

Figure 19
.
The local transformation of an edge following Bouttier-Di Francesco-Guitter’s bijection: the non-geodesic case (left), and the geodesic case (right).

For sake of simplicity, we assume that each corner incident to a square vertex inherits the label of that vertex. The
successor of a corner
labeled
\ell\geq 2
is defined as the first corner labeled
\ell-1
encountered when moving clockwise around the tree. The
successor of a flag

\ell\geq 1
is defined as the first corner labeled
\ell
encountered when moving clockwise around the tree.
Conversely, the
predecessors
of a corner are the corners and flags for which it is the successor.

Recall the definition and properties of Eulerian maps from Section
1.1.2
. In particular, every Eulerian map endowed with one of its proper 2-colorings of its faces, possesses a canonical orientation of its edges by requiring that its white faces lie on the left of any directed edge.
Given a pointed bicolored Eulerian planar map, we label its vertices by their geodesic labeling, see Section
A.1.1
.
Then, we define the transformation
\Phi_{\mathrm{BDG}}
by applying to every edge, the local transformation illustrated in Figure
19
.

One of the main results of
[
19
]
is the following:

Theorem B.6

(
[
19
]
, Section 3.1)
.

The application
\Phi_{\mathrm{BDG}}
is a bijection between the set of pointed Eulerian planar maps and the set of labeled mobiles.

Remark B.7
.

The bijective mapping
\Phi_{\mathrm{BDG}}
induces several parameter correspondences. Let
\mathrm{m}
be a pointed Eulerian planar map, and write
\mathrm{t}=\Phi_{\mathrm{BDG}}(\mathrm{m})
. Then, we have the following correspondences between:

•

the degree of the pointed vertex of
\mathrm{m}
and the number of corners with label
1
plus the number of flags with label
0
in
\mathrm{t}
,

•

the degree of the white faces of
\mathrm{m}
and the white (round) vertex degrees in
\mathrm{t}
,

•

the directed geodesic labels of
\mathrm{m}
and the labels of the square vertices in
\mathrm{t}
.

Remark B.8
.

Note that if the white vertex degree of
\mathrm{t}
is at most
d\geq 1
, then
\mathrm{m}
has white face degree at most
d
. Hence, the directed geodesic labeling between two adjacent vertices of
\mathrm{m}
is at most
d-1
, since the contour of their adjacent white face forms a directed path connecting them.

It will be important for the following to observe that, this implies that the labels of both flags on an edge of
\mathrm{t}
can differ by at most
d-1
.

This remark justifies the following definition. A
d
-labeled mobile
is a labeled mobile with maximum white vertex degree
d
.

B.3.2.
d
-labeled mobiles are
d
-blossoming mobiles

In this section, we prove that for every bipartite plane map
\mathrm{m}
(with its minimal
\alpha_{d}
-orientation
\mathcal{O})
, the blossoming mobile
\mathrm{t}:=\Phi_{\mathrm{BF}}(\mathrm{m},\mathcal{O})
is close to the labeled mobile
\tilde{\mathrm{t}}:=\Phi_{\mathrm{BDG}}(\mathrm{m}^{\dagger})
. Specifically, the vertices and edges of
\mathrm{t}
and
\tilde{\mathrm{t}}
coincide, and the labels of
\tilde{\mathrm{t}}
are replaced by some orientation on
\mathrm{t}
.

More precisely, for
d\geq 1
, we define an operation
\Upsilon_{d}
on
d
-labeled mobiles as follows. Fix
\mathrm{t}
a
d
-labeled mobile. First, add to each corner of
\mathrm{t}
incident to a square vertex as many opening stems as it possesses predecessors. Then, endow the resulting tree with the orientation
\mathcal{O}:\mathrm{H}(t)\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
defined by:

•

For every white-square edge
e=\left\{h_{\circ},h_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{262144}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\hbox to5.92pt{\vbox to5.92pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 2.1056pt\lower-2.1056pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{
{}{{}}{}
{}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -2.36 -2.36 M -2.36 -2.36 L -2.36 4.72 L 4.72 4.72 L 4.72 -2.36 Z M 4.72 4.72}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }

, set
\mathcal{O}(h_{\circ})\coloneqq 0
and
\mathcal{O}(h_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{262144}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\hbox to5.92pt{\vbox to5.92pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 2.1056pt\lower-2.1056pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{

.

•

For every white-black edge
e=\left\{h_{\circ},h_{\bullet}\right\}
with flags
\ell_{1}
and
\ell_{2}
, in the clockwise order around the white vertex, set
\mathcal{O}(h_{\circ})\coloneqq\ell_{2}-\ell_{1}+1
and
\mathcal{O}(h_{\bullet})\coloneqq d+1-\mathcal{O}(h_{\circ})
. Observe that, as a directly consequence of Condition (III) in the definition of labeled mobiles and of Remark
B.8
, one has
\mathcal{O}(h_{\circ})\geq 1
and
\mathcal{O}(h_{\bullet})\geq 0
.

Finally, forget about the flags and the vertex labeling. The resulting tree is denoted by
\Upsilon_{d}(\mathrm{t})
. Then, we have:

Lemma B.9
.

Fix
d\geq 1
. For any
d
-labeled mobile
\mathrm{t}
,
\Upsilon_{d}(\mathrm{t})
is a
d
-blossoming mobile.

This leads us to the following result, illustrated in Figure
20
:

Proposition B.10
.

Fix
d\geq 1
. On the set of pointed Eulerian planar maps with maximum white vertex degree
d
, we have:

\Phi_{\mathrm{BF}}\circ{\mathrm{Dual}}=\Upsilon_{d}\circ\Phi_{\mathrm{BDG}}.

(B.3)

Figure 20
.
A bipartite plane map and its successive transformations illustrating that Bernardi-Fusy’s bijective scheme, when applied to
\alpha_{d}
-orientations, coincides with Bouttier-Di Francesco-Guitter’s bijection.

We begin by proving the proposition, relying on Lemma
B.9
.

Proof.

We first show that
\Upsilon_{d}
is an injective mapping from the set of
d
-labeled mobiles into the set of
d
-blossoming mobiles. Let
\mathrm{t}
be a
d
-labeled mobile, and denote
\tilde{\mathrm{t}}=\Upsilon_{d}(\mathrm{t})
. Since both mobiles share the same underlying undecorated tree, reconstructing
\mathrm{t}
from
\tilde{\mathrm{t}}
reduces to recovering the value of its labels.

The key idea is that it suffices to reconstruct the successor function on
\mathrm{t}
from
\tilde{\mathrm{t}}
.
Indeed, corners with no successor have label
1
, and since the successor of a corner labeled
l
is a corner labeled
l-1
, we can recover the other corner labels iteratively. Similarly, flags with no successor have label
0
, and otherwise they have the same label as their successors, which are corners by definition.

Now, from the opening stems of
\tilde{\mathrm{t}}
, we can reconstruct the predecessor function: the counterclockwise cyclic contour of
\tilde{\mathrm{t}}
induces a matching between the opening stems and the set formed by the sides of the round–round edges together with the square corners that precede a round–square edge in the clockwise direction. Then, we can recover the successor function from the predecessor function. Therefore, we conclude that
\Upsilon_{d}
is injective.

Secondly, for any
n\geq 1
, the map
\Phi_{\mathrm{BF}}\circ\mathrm{Dual}\circ{\Phi_{\mathrm{BDG}}}^{-1}
induces a bijection between the (finite) set of
d
-labeled mobiles with
n
vertices and the (finite) set of
d
-blossoming mobiles with
n
vertices. Hence,
\Upsilon_{d}
defines a bijection between these two sets.

To conclude, it remains to check that the restriction of
\Upsilon_{d}
to the (finite) set of
d
-labeled mobiles with
n
vertices coincides with
\Phi_{\mathrm{BF}}\circ\mathrm{Dual}\circ{\Phi_{\mathrm{BDG}}}^{-1}
. This follows from the fact that the closure operations
{\Phi_{\mathrm{BF}}}^{-1}
and
{\Phi_{\mathrm{BDG}}}^{-1}
both depend only on the successor function (see
[
9
, Section 3]
and
[
19
, Section 3.2]
, respectively), and that we proved that these functions coincide for any labeled mobile
\mathrm{t}
and its associated blossoming mobile
\Upsilon(\mathrm{t})
.
∎

Proof of Lemma
B.9
.

In this proof, Roman numerals refer to the conditions in the definition of labeled mobiles, while the Arabic numerals to those in the definition of
d
-blossoming mobiles given at the beginning of Section
B.2
. Let
\mathrm{t}
be a
d
-labeled mobile, and let
\tilde{\mathrm{t}}\coloneqq\Upsilon_{d}(\mathrm{t})
. We prove that
\tilde{\mathrm{t}}
verifies the conditions (1) to (6).

First, recall that the excess of a blossoming mobile is defined as the difference between the number of half-edges incident to round vertices and the number of opening stems. Next, the number of half-edges incident to round vertices in
\tilde{\mathrm{t}}
is the same as in
\mathrm{t}
, and the latter is exactly equal to the number of its flags plus the number of its corners incident to square vertices. Therefore, the excess of
\tilde{\mathrm{t}}
is equal to the number of flags with label 0 (which are exactly the flags with no successor in the mobile by (I) and (II)) plus the number of corner labeled
1
(which are exactly the labeled corner with no successor in the mobile by (II)) in
\mathrm{t}
. Since there exists at least one flag labeled
0
by (I), Condition (1) holds.

Conditions (2) and (3) are direct consequences of the definition of
\Upsilon_{d}
, which preserves the shape of
\mathrm{t}
and the type of its vertices.

Next, let
v
be a white vertex of
\mathrm{t}
. Let
m
and
n
be the number of white-square and white-black edges incident to
v
, respectively. List the white-black edges as
e_{1},\ldots,e_{n}
, in clockwise order around
v
, starting arbitrarily from any one of them. For any
1\leq i\leq n
, denote by
\ell^{\text{left}}_{i},\ell^{\text{right}}_{i}
the labels of the flags situated on the left and on the right of
e_{i}
.

Since both mobiles
\mathrm{t}
and
\tilde{\mathrm{t}}
share the same underlying undecorated tree, we will also refer to
v
and
e_{1},\ldots,e_{n}
for their corresponding vertex and edges in
\tilde{\mathrm{t}}
.
With these notations, the orientation
\mathcal{O}
of
\tilde{\mathrm{t}}
satisfies the following:

\mathcal{O}(h_{i})=\ell^{\text{right}}_{i}-\ell^{\text{left}}_{i}+1,

for every
1\leq i\leq n
, and where
h_{i}
is the half-edge of
e_{i}
incident to
v
. Moreover, note that
\ell^{\text{right}}_{i}
and
\ell^{\text{left}}_{i+1}
are consecutive labels of flags around
v
in the clockwise order in
\mathrm{t}
. Then, it follows from (IV) that
\ell^{\text{right}}_{i}-\ell^{\text{left}}_{i+1}
is the number of white-square edges between
e_{i}
and
e_{i+1}
in
\mathrm{t}
. Thus, it is also the case for
\tilde{\mathrm{t}}
. Therefore, by rewriting the following sum, we obtain:

\mathrm{deg_{out}}(v)=\sum\limits_{1\leq i\leq n}{(\ell^{\text{right}}_{i}-\ell^{\text{left}}_{i}+1)}=n+\sum\limits_{1\leq i\leq n}{(\ell^{\text{right}}_{i}-\ell^{\text{left}}_{i+1})}=n+m=\deg(v)

where
\ell^{\text{left}}_{n+1}\coloneqq\ell^{\text{left}}_{1}
. Finally, as the orientation
\mathcal{O}
is a
(d+1)
-fractional orientation, one has
{\mathrm{deg_{in}}(v)=d\cdot\deg(v)}
. Hence, (4) follows.

Due to comparable considerations for black vertices, (III) implies (5).
Finally, as shown at the beginning of this proof,
\mathcal{O}(h_{\circ})>0
for any white-black edge
e=\left\{h_{\circ},h_{\bullet}\right\}
. In particular, this implies (6).
∎
